│ Tools       │ 🔧 sequentialthinking

│             │ │ operation more                                 │

│             │ │ tokens, torch.ones_like(tokens))\n```\n\nThe   │ {
│             │ │ issue is that `torch.ones_like(tokens)`        │   "thought_nu
│             │ │ creates a new tensor with the same shape as    │   "total_thou
│             │ │ `tokens` (which is ). This is wasteful         │   "next_thoug
│             │ │ because:\n\n1. We're creating a tensor of size │   "branches":
│             │ │ [32, 128] just to fill it with ones\n2. The    │   "thought_hi
│             │ │ scatter_add_ operation then uses this tensor   │ }

│             │ │ This creates unnecessary memory allocation and │
│             │ │ copy operations\n\nThe optimization should be  │
│             │ │ to use a scalar value or a more efficient      │
│             │ │ approach. Looking at PyTorch's scatter_add_    │
│             │ │ documentation, we can actually pass a scalar   │
│             │ │ value directly if we use the right             │
│             │ │ approach.\n\nHowever, looking more carefully   │
│             │ │ at the semantics of scatter_add_, the third    │
│             │ │ argument should have the same shape as the     │
│             │ │ index tensor. So we can't just pass a          │
│             │ │ scalar.\n\nBut wait - there's another          │
│             │ │ approach! We can use `scatter_add_` with a     │
│             │ │ scalar by using a different pattern. Or we can │
│             │ │ avoid creating the full tensor by using a more │
│             │ │ efficient method.\n\nActually, looking at the  │
│             │ │ PyTorch documentation more carefully,          │
│             │ │ `scatter_add_` expects the src tensor to have  │
│             │ │ the same shape as the index tensor. So we need │
│             │ │ to create a tensor of ones, but we can         │
│             │ │ optimize how we create it.\n\nOne optimization │

│             │ │ scatter_add_.\n\nLet me think about this       │

│             │ │ with the same shape as `tokens`. If `tokens`   │
│             │ │ is , this creates a tensor of that size just   │
│             │ │ to add 1 to each position.\n\nHowever, looking │
│             │ │ at the PyTorch documentation for               │
│             │ │ `scatter_add_`, the src tensor must have the   │
│             │ │ same shape as the index tensor. So we can\'t   │
│             │ │ avoid creating a tensor of the same            │
│             │ │ shape.\n\nBut wait - we can optimize this by   │
│             │ │ creating the ones tensor more efficiently.     │
│             │ │ Instead of `torch.ones_like(tokens)`, we could │
│             │ │ use:\n- `torch.ones(tokens.shape,              │

│             │ │ different algorithm altogether\n\nActually,    │

│             │ │ repetition_penalties)\n                        │

│             │ │ torch.cuda.is_available() else None\n    end = │
│             │ │ time.time()\n    \n    return (end - start) /  │
│             │ │ iterations * 1000  # Convert to ms\n\n\ndef    │
│             │ │ run_benchmarks():\n    """Run performance      │

│             │ │ {'thought': 'Looking at the                    │ Sequential th
│             │ │ `get_token_bin_counts_and_mask` function       │
│             │ │ again:\n\n```python\ndef                       │ Status:
│             │ │ get_token_bin_counts_and_mask(\n    tokens:    │ {
│             │ │ torch.Tensor,\n    vocab_size: int,\n          │   "thought_nu
│             │ │ num_seqs: int,\n) -> Tuple:\n    bin_counts =  │   "total_thou
│             │ │ torch.zeros((num_seqs, vocab_size + 1),\n      │   "next_thoug
│             │ │ dtype=torch.long,\n                            │   "branches":
│             │ │ device=tokens.device)\n                        │   "thought_hi
│             │ │ bin_counts.scatter_add_(1, tokens,             │ }
│             │ │ torch.ones_like(tokens))\n    bin_counts =     │
│             │ │ bin_counts[:, :vocab_size]\n    mask =         │
│             │ │ bin_counts > 0\n\n    return bin_counts,       │
│             │ │ mask\n```\n\nThe issue is that                 │
│             │ │ `torch.ones_like(tokens)` creates a tensor     │
│             │ │ with the same shape as `tokens`. If `tokens`   │
│             │ │ is , this creates a tensor of that             │
│             │ │ size.\n\nHowever, looking at the PyTorch       │
│             │ │ documentation for `scatter_add_`, the src      │
│             │ │ tensor must have the same shape as the index   │
│             │ │ tensor. So we can\'t avoid creating a tensor   │
│             │ │ of the same shape.\n\nBut we can optimize by   │
│             │ │ using `torch.ones` with explicit shape instead │
│             │ │ of `torch.ones_like`, which might be slightly  │
│             │ │ more                                           │
│             │ │ efficient:\n\n```python\nbin_counts.scatter_a… │
│             │ │ tokens, torch.ones(tokens.shape,               │
│             │ │ dtype=tokens.dtype,                            │
│             │ │ device=tokens.device))\n```\n\nOr even better, │
│             │ │ we can use a 1D tensor and reshape             │
│             │ │ it:\n\n```python\nbin_counts.scatter_add_(1,   │
│             │ │ tokens, torch.ones(tokens.numel(),             │
│             │ │ dtype=torch.long,                              │
│             │ │ device=tokens.device).reshape_as(tokens))\n``… │
│             │ │ actually, looking at the performance test      │
│             │ │ results, the `get_token_bin_counts_and_mask`   │
│             │ │ function is already quite fast (0.672 ms for   │
│             │ │ 32x128 tokens). The main bottleneck is in      │
│             │ │ `apply_penalties`.\n\nHowever, for             │
│             │ │ completeness and to follow the reference       │
│             │ │ commit message about "remove synchronizing     │
│             │ │ operation", let me check if there\'s a better  │
│             │ │ way.\n\nActually, I realize that the main      │
│             │ │ issue might not be in the memory allocation    │
│             │ │ but in the synchronization that can happen     │
│             │ │ with scatter operations. On GPU, scatter       │
│             │ │ operations can cause synchronization because   │
│             │ │ they need to coordinate across threads.\n\nOne │
│             │ │ optimization is to use a different algorithm   │
│             │ │ altogether. For example, we could use          │
│             │ │ `torch.bincount` which might be more efficient │
│             │ │ on GPU. But `torch.bincount` doesn\'t support  │
│             │ │ multi-dimensional input directly, so we\'d     │
│             │ │ need to use a loop or other approach.\n\nLet   │
│             │ │ me check if there\'s a more efficient way to   │
│             │ │ compute bin counts on GPU. Actually, looking   │
│             │ │ at the code, I think the current approach is   │
│             │ │ reasonable for GPU. The main optimization      │
│             │ │ we\'ve made is in `apply_penalties`, which is  │
│             │ │ where the majority of the time is              │

│             │ │ Optimization:**\n- **Before:** The function    │
│             │ │ computed `torch.where(prompt_mask |            │
│             │ │ output_mask, repetition_penalties, 1.0)`       │
│             │ │ twice, once for positive logits and once for   │
│             │ │ negative logits\n- **After:** The function     │

│             │ │ then reuses them for both operations\n\n**Code │
│             │ │ Changes:**\n```python\n# Before (lines         │
│             │ │ 50-53):\nlogits /= torch.where(prompt_mask |   │

│             │ │ once to avoid redundant                        │
│             │ │ computation\nrepetition_mask = prompt_mask |   │
│             │ │ output_mask\nrepetition_factor =               │
│             │ │ torch.where(repetition_mask,                   │
│             │ │ repetition_penalties, 1.0)\n\n# Apply          │
│             │ │ repetition penalties\nlogits /=                │
│             │ │ repetition_factor\nlogits *=                   │
│             │ │ repetition_factor\n```\n\n## Performance       │
│             │ │ Impact\n\n### CPU Benchmarks\n- **Small batch  │
│             │ │ (32x100):** 5.0% improvement\n- **Medium batch │

│             │ │ (32x32000):** Slight regression (-2.0%) due to │
│             │ │ CPU-specific overhead\n- **Average             │
│             │ │ improvement:** 2.0% across all                 │
│             │ │ configurations\n\n### GPU Expected Impact\nThe │
